[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Major Depressive Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you noticed a change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Sleep has gotten… broken, I guess is the best way to put it.\n\nI don’t usually have one solid stretch of sleep anymore. I’ll fall asleep for a little while, then come up again, then drift off again, almost in bits and pieces. And then very early, before it’s really morning—before dawn a lot of the time—I wake up and my mind is already going. Not necessarily in an organized way, just turning things over, and once that starts I usually can’t settle back down.\n\nI’ll lie there trying to be still, telling myself there’s no point getting up yet, but the day has sort of already begun in my head. By the time it’s actually morning, I feel like I never really got properly rested.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up feeling at your lowest, is your first thought usually about your sister being gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s usually that clear.\n\nSometimes, yes, my sister is there in my mind right away—or not even a thought exactly, more like I wake up and there’s this awareness that she’s still gone, if that makes sense. Like I haven’t had time to think anything yet, but the fact of it is already sitting there.\n\nBut other mornings it’s more vague than that. I wake up low, and it takes me a little while to know what I’m even feeling or why. It’s not always a sentence in my head like, “My sister is gone.” Sometimes it’s just heaviness first, and then the thoughts come in afterward.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say yes, that’s always or usually my first thought. It can be part of it, but I don’t know that I’ve tracked it closely enough to answer more definitely.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think guilt more than worthless, though sometimes the guilt kind of turns into feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nI keep going back over those last weeks with my sister, almost like I’m reviewing a tape in my head. I’ll think, why didn’t I call that day, or why didn’t I stay on the phone longer, or why didn’t I ask better questions? I tell myself I should have listened more carefully, or said something more comforting, or found some more useful way to help her instead of just doing whatever I did at the time.\n\nAnd part of me knows—at least rationally—that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I do know that. If someone else said these things to me, I’d probably tell them they were being too hard on themselves. But when it’s me, it doesn’t seem to land the same way. I keep thinking there must have been something I missed, something I should have done differently.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling guilty. Excessively, maybe… I don’t know. It feels excessive when I say it out loud, but it still feels very real when I’m in it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you feel guilty, is it mostly about your sister and what happened around her death?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep going over the last weeks with my sister and telling myself I should have called more, listened better, done something more useful, even when part of me knows I couldn't have changed what happened.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, mostly. I mean, that’s where my mind goes with it almost every time.

It’s those last weeks with my sister that I keep replaying. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or lying awake, and suddenly I’m back there thinking, I should have called her more. Or I should have stayed on the phone longer when I did call. I should have listened better, picked up on something, asked the right question, done something actually useful instead of just… whatever I thought was enough at the time.

And I do understand, at least in one part of my mind, that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I can say that out loud. But it doesn’t stop the other part from going over it again and again, like if I review it carefully enough I’ll find the moment where I failed her. So yes, it’s mostly tied to her, and to what happened around her death.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, I suppose because I got frightened by my own thoughts. Some mornings, when I wake up, or even before I’m fully out of bed, I find myself thinking it might be easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Not that I have a plan to do anything to myself—I don’t—but the thought is there, and it’s been unsettling enough that I felt I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nI almost didn’t make the appointment because part of me kept saying, “Oh, you’re just being dramatic,” or “Everyone has low days,” but it’s the fact that it keeps coming back in the mornings that worries me. I don’t want to hurt myself, but I also don’t feel right pretending those thoughts aren’t happening. So… that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer “most days,” if I’m honest. I know that sounds evasive, but I don’t mean it that way. I’ve certainly had mornings where I wake up with this heavy, low feeling, and then I spend part of the day sort of moving through things without much brightness in me.\n\nBut whether it’s truly most days, I haven’t been keeping track in any careful way. Some days I can still function and seem fairly normal from the outside, so I start questioning whether I’m really “depressed” or just having a rough patch. I suppose I’d say I’ve been feeling down more often than I’d like, but I’m not sure I can confidently say exactly how many days.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—very suddenly, unexpectedly—and since then I haven’t really felt like I’ve gotten my footing back.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself that of course I’m upset, anyone would be, but it’s been about four weeks now and the heaviness has sort of stayed with me. I don’t mean every single minute of every day, but since her death, that’s when I started noticing this change in myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” no. I mean, I do worry, but I don’t know that it’s this broad, constant worrying about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nLately my thoughts tend to circle around certain things, and then I second-guess myself about whether that counts as excessive or whether it’s just… understandable. I wouldn’t say I’m lying awake making lists of every possible disaster, but I also don’t feel especially settled. It’s a little hard for me to separate worry from just feeling unsettled and sad, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is: not really a wide range of worries, at least not that I’ve noticed. But I may need you to clarify what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I’m still doing some of the things I normally do, so from the outside it may not look like I’ve stopped enjoying life exactly. I still make myself go to book club, for example, and I’ve gone to the dance night a couple of times because I don’t want to disappear or have people worry about me.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s like I’m not fully in the room. I’m sitting there while everyone is talking or laughing, and I feel sort of behind a pane of glass, if that makes sense. I can see that these are the things I used to enjoy, and I know how I’m supposed to feel, but I don’t really get that same pleasure from them.\n\nEven smaller things at home… my garden used to give me such a lift, just checking on what was coming up or fussing over the plants. And I used to really enjoy cooking something for the neighbors, especially if I had extra soup or a casserole. Lately I can still do those things, but they feel flatter. They don’t give me that little bit of warmth or satisfaction the way they used to. So yes, I suppose the interest is still there in a dutiful way, but the pleasure part has been missing.\n\nDoctor: When you feel at your lowest, are your thoughts mainly about missing your sister and her death?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to separate it out, honestly. A lot of the lowest moments do seem to have her in them, or at least her death is sort of the doorway into them, if that makes sense. I’ll think about something ordinary and then suddenly remember she isn’t here, and that can pull me down very quickly.\n\nBut I don’t know that I can say my thoughts are mainly only about missing her. Sometimes it feels more like this heavy feeling settles over everything, and then I may not even be thinking one clear thought. It’s more like… I’m just low, and if I try to trace it back, yes, her being gone is there. But it isn’t always a specific memory or a specific thought like “I miss my sister” running through my mind.\n\nSo I guess I’d say her death is very tied up in it, but when I’m at my lowest, it can feel broader and harder to name than just missing her.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have there still been moments when something can genuinely lift your mood, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean, there are moments where I can tell something should lift my mood, if that makes sense. Like I’ll still make myself go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night because I know it’s better than just staying home and withdrawing completely. And sometimes people are kind, or someone says something funny, and I can sort of recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to. I end up sitting there feeling a bit like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else be part of it. I’m present, but I’m not really… warmed by it, I suppose.\n\nEven with things that used to reliably give me a little lift, like being out in my garden or cooking something for the neighbors, it’s flatter now. I still do them sometimes, but that little spark of pleasure or satisfaction just doesn’t come the same way. So if my mood lifts, it’s brief and kind of faint, not the genuine lift I remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this low even when you are not thinking about your sister?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I wish I could give you a cleaner answer, but it’s hard for me to untangle it in the moment.\n\nThere are times when I’m low and I don’t think I’m consciously thinking, “This is about my sister,” or picturing her exactly. It can just feel like the day has gone dim somehow, and I’m moving through it with that heaviness there. But then if I stop and examine it, I wonder if she’s still underneath it in some way, even if I wasn’t actively thinking about her.\n\nSo… yes, I think I can feel low even when she isn’t right at the front of my mind. But I also don’t know how much she’s still in the background, if that makes sense. It’s not always a clear thought about her, but it may still be connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings begin only after your sister died?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, really only after she died. It’s been about a month now, maybe just a little over or under depending on how I count it, but that’s when all of this started in this way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad periods in life before, of course—at my age you don’t get through everything untouched—but this particular heaviness, the way it’s been sitting on me day after day, began after my sister died unexpectedly. It felt like one day life was difficult but still recognizable, and then after that call, or after realizing she was really gone, everything shifted. Since then it’s just been… about a month of feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have there been any moments when you truly felt better for a while, even if only briefly?\n\nPatient: Only in a very muted sort of way. I still make myself go to things, like book club or the dance night, because part of me knows those are the things that used to help me feel more like myself. And I don’t want to just disappear into the house and stop doing everything.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s not the same. I can be sitting with people I like, hearing the conversation, maybe even knowing something is funny or pleasant, but it feels like it doesn’t quite get through to me. Almost like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching it happen rather than really being in it.\n\nAnd that’s true at home too. My garden used to give me such a little lift, even just checking on things or pulling a few weeds, and cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and warm inside. Lately I still do those things sometimes, but the feeling I used to get from them just… doesn’t arrive. So I suppose there are small moments where I’m distracted or a bit steadier, but truly better? Not really, not in the way I remember.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one, no—not like this, and not clearly for two weeks or longer.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches. There were times when I felt discouraged or worn down, or when life felt lonely or disappointing for a while. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve never been sad before, because that wouldn’t be true. But when you ask “depressed like this,” I have trouble saying yes, because this feels different in its weight and how it’s stayed with me.\n\nSo… as best as I can answer, no, I don’t think I’ve had another period before this where I felt this way for two weeks or more. Not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you felt down or had little pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. It’s not always the same intensity every hour, but it’s been there most days in one form or another.\n\nI still make myself show up for things, like book club or the dance night, because I don’t want to just stop living my life completely, and those things used to mean something to me. But lately I’ll be sitting there, with people talking around me, and I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass. I can see that I’m supposed to be enjoying it, or I remember that I used to enjoy it, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nAnd it’s the same with the smaller things at home. My garden used to give me this little lift, even just looking at what was coming up or fussing with the plants. Cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and connected. Now I still do some of it, but the pleasure just isn’t there the way it was. So yes, I’d say it’s been nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you noticed a change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Sleep has gotten… broken, I guess is the best way to put it.\n\nI don’t usually have one solid stretch of sleep anymore. I’ll fall asleep for a little while, then come up again, then drift off again, almost in bits and pieces. And then very early, before it’s really morning—before dawn a lot of the time—I wake up and my mind is already going. Not necessarily in an organized way, just turning things over, and once that starts I usually can’t settle back down.\n\nI’ll lie there trying to be still, telling myself there’s no point getting up yet, but the day has sort of already begun in my head. By the time it’s actually morning, I feel like I never really got properly rested.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up feeling at your lowest, is your first thought usually about your sister being gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s usually that clear.\n\nSometimes, yes, my sister is there in my mind right away—or not even a thought exactly, more like I wake up and there’s this awareness that she’s still gone, if that makes sense. Like I haven’t had time to think anything yet, but the fact of it is already sitting there.\n\nBut other mornings it’s more vague than that. I wake up low, and it takes me a little while to know what I’m even feeling or why. It’s not always a sentence in my head like, “My sister is gone.” Sometimes it’s just heaviness first, and then the thoughts come in afterward.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say yes, that’s always or usually my first thought. It can be part of it, but I don’t know that I’ve tracked it closely enough to answer more definitely.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think guilt more than worthless, though sometimes the guilt kind of turns into feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nI keep going back over those last weeks with my sister, almost like I’m reviewing a tape in my head. I’ll think, why didn’t I call that day, or why didn’t I stay on the phone longer, or why didn’t I ask better questions? I tell myself I should have listened more carefully, or said something more comforting, or found some more useful way to help her instead of just doing whatever I did at the time.\n\nAnd part of me knows—at least rationally—that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I do know that. If someone else said these things to me, I’d probably tell them they were being too hard on themselves. But when it’s me, it doesn’t seem to land the same way. I keep thinking there must have been something I missed, something I should have done differently.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling guilty. Excessively, maybe… I don’t know. It feels excessive when I say it out loud, but it still feels very real when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel guilty, is it mostly about your sister and what happened around her death?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. I mean, that’s where my mind goes with it almost every time.\n\nIt’s those last weeks with my sister that I keep replaying. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or lying awake, and suddenly I’m back there thinking, I should have called her more. Or I should have stayed on the phone longer when I did call. I should have listened better, picked up on something, asked the right question, done something actually useful instead of just… whatever I thought was enough at the time.\n\nAnd I do understand, at least in one part of my mind, that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I can say that out loud. But it doesn’t stop the other part from going over it again and again, like if I review it carefully enough I’ll find the moment where I failed her. So yes, it’s mostly tied to her, and to what happened around her death.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s been about a month now, maybe just a little over or under depending on how I count it, but that’s when all of this started in this way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad periods in life before, of course—at my age you don’t get through everything untouched—but this particular heaviness, the way it’s been sitting on me day after day, began after my sister died unexpectedly. It felt like one day life was difficult but still recognizable, and then after that call, or after realizing she was really gone, everything shifted. Since then it’s just been… about a month of feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have there been any moments when you truly felt better for a while, even if only briefly?\n\nPatient: Only in a very muted sort of way. I still make myself go to things, like book club or the dance night, because part of me knows those are the things that used to help me feel more like myself. And I don’t want to just disappear into the house and stop doing everything.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s not the same. I can be sitting with people I like, hearing the conversation, maybe even knowing something is funny or pleasant, but it feels like it doesn’t quite get through to me. Almost like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching it happen rather than really being in it.\n\nAnd that’s true at home too. My garden used to give me such a little lift, even just checking on things or pulling a few weeds, and cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and warm inside. Lately I still do those things sometimes, but the feeling I used to get from them just… doesn’t arrive. So I suppose there are small moments where I’m distracted or a bit steadier, but truly better? Not really, not in the way I remember.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one, no—not like this, and not clearly for two weeks or longer.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches. There were times when I felt discouraged or worn down, or when life felt lonely or disappointing for a while. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve never been sad before, because that wouldn’t be true. But when you ask “depressed like this,” I have trouble saying yes, because this feels different in its weight and how it’s stayed with me.\n\nSo… as best as I can answer, no, I don’t think I’ve had another period before this where I felt this way for two weeks or more. Not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you felt down or had little pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. It’s not always the same intensity every hour, but it’s been there most days in one form or another.\n\nI still make myself show up for things, like book club or the dance night, because I don’t want to just stop living my life completely, and those things used to mean something to me. But lately I’ll be sitting there, with people talking around me, and I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass. I can see that I’m supposed to be enjoying it, or I remember that I used to enjoy it, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nAnd it’s the same with the smaller things at home. My garden used to give me this little lift, even just looking at what was coming up or fussing with the plants. Cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and connected. Now I still do some of it, but the pleasure just isn’t there the way it was. So yes, I’d say it’s been nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you noticed a change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Sleep has gotten… broken, I guess is the best way to put it.\n\nI don’t usually have one solid stretch of sleep anymore. I’ll fall asleep for a little while, then come up again, then drift off again, almost in bits and pieces. And then very early, before it’s really morning—before dawn a lot of the time—I wake up and my mind is already going. Not necessarily in an organized way, just turning things over, and once that starts I usually can’t settle back down.\n\nI’ll lie there trying to be still, telling myself there’s no point getting up yet, but the day has sort of already begun in my head. By the time it’s actually morning, I feel like I never really got properly rested.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up feeling at your lowest, is your first thought usually about your sister being gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s usually that clear.\n\nSometimes, yes, my sister is there in my mind right away—or not even a thought exactly, more like I wake up and there’s this awareness that she’s still gone, if that makes sense. Like I haven’t had time to think anything yet, but the fact of it is already sitting there.\n\nBut other mornings it’s more vague than that. I wake up low, and it takes me a little while to know what I’m even feeling or why. It’s not always a sentence in my head like, “My sister is gone.” Sometimes it’s just heaviness first, and then the thoughts come in afterward.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say yes, that’s always or usually my first thought. It can be part of it, but I don’t know that I’ve tracked it closely enough to answer more definitely.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think guilt more than worthless, though sometimes the guilt kind of turns into feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nI keep going back over those last weeks with my sister, almost like I’m reviewing a tape in my head. I’ll think, why didn’t I call that day, or why didn’t I stay on the phone longer, or why didn’t I ask better questions? I tell myself I should have listened more carefully, or said something more comforting, or found some more useful way to help her instead of just doing whatever I did at the time.\n\nAnd part of me knows—at least rationally—that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I do know that. If someone else said these things to me, I’d probably tell them they were being too hard on themselves. But when it’s me, it doesn’t seem to land the same way. I keep thinking there must have been something I missed, something I should have done differently.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling guilty. Excessively, maybe… I don’t know. It feels excessive when I say it out loud, but it still feels very real when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel guilty, is it mostly about your sister and what happened around her death?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. I mean, that’s where my mind goes with it almost every time.\n\nIt’s those last weeks with my sister that I keep replaying. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or lying awake, and suddenly I’m back there thinking, I should have called her more. Or I should have stayed on the phone longer when I did call. I should have listened better, picked up on something, asked the right question, done something actually useful instead of just… whatever I thought was enough at the time.\n\nAnd I do understand, at least in one part of my mind, that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I can say that out loud. But it doesn’t stop the other part from going over it again and again, like if I review it carefully enough I’ll find the moment where I failed her. So yes, it’s mostly tied to her, and to what happened around her death.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sometimes it does, a little, but not the way it used to. That’s the part that’s been unsettling, honestly.

I still make myself go to things. Like I’ll go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night if I said I would, because I know if I stop going entirely that probably isn’t good for me. And sometimes something nice is happening right in front of me—someone says something funny, or everyone’s talking about a book, or the music is on—and I can recognize, intellectually, that this is pleasant. I can even smile or laugh in the right place.

But inside it’s… muted. Like I’m there, but I’m behind a pane of glass watching it instead of really being in it. The good thing happens, and I see that it’s good, but it doesn’t quite reach me.

Even things that used to give me that small lift—my garden, especially, or cooking something for the neighbors—lately I still do them, but the little spark I used to get from them isn’t really there. I might be glad I did it afterward in a kind of dutiful way, but it doesn’t brighten me the way it used to. So yes, maybe there’s a slight lift sometimes, but it feels very brief and far away.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, I suppose because I got frightened by my own thoughts. Some mornings, when I wake up, or even before I’m fully out of bed, I find myself thinking it might be easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Not that I have a plan to do anything to myself—I don’t—but the thought is there, and it’s been unsettling enough that I felt I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nI almost didn’t make the appointment because part of me kept saying, “Oh, you’re just being dramatic,” or “Everyone has low days,” but it’s the fact that it keeps coming back in the mornings that worries me. I don’t want to hurt myself, but I also don’t feel right pretending those thoughts aren’t happening. So… that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer “most days,” if I’m honest. I know that sounds evasive, but I don’t mean it that way. I’ve certainly had mornings where I wake up with this heavy, low feeling, and then I spend part of the day sort of moving through things without much brightness in me.\n\nBut whether it’s truly most days, I haven’t been keeping track in any careful way. Some days I can still function and seem fairly normal from the outside, so I start questioning whether I’m really “depressed” or just having a rough patch. I suppose I’d say I’ve been feeling down more often than I’d like, but I’m not sure I can confidently say exactly how many days.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—very suddenly, unexpectedly—and since then I haven’t really felt like I’ve gotten my footing back.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself that of course I’m upset, anyone would be, but it’s been about four weeks now and the heaviness has sort of stayed with me. I don’t mean every single minute of every day, but since her death, that’s when I started noticing this change in myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” no. I mean, I do worry, but I don’t know that it’s this broad, constant worrying about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nLately my thoughts tend to circle around certain things, and then I second-guess myself about whether that counts as excessive or whether it’s just… understandable. I wouldn’t say I’m lying awake making lists of every possible disaster, but I also don’t feel especially settled. It’s a little hard for me to separate worry from just feeling unsettled and sad, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is: not really a wide range of worries, at least not that I’ve noticed. But I may need you to clarify what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I’m still doing some of the things I normally do, so from the outside it may not look like I’ve stopped enjoying life exactly. I still make myself go to book club, for example, and I’ve gone to the dance night a couple of times because I don’t want to disappear or have people worry about me.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s like I’m not fully in the room. I’m sitting there while everyone is talking or laughing, and I feel sort of behind a pane of glass, if that makes sense. I can see that these are the things I used to enjoy, and I know how I’m supposed to feel, but I don’t really get that same pleasure from them.\n\nEven smaller things at home… my garden used to give me such a lift, just checking on what was coming up or fussing over the plants. And I used to really enjoy cooking something for the neighbors, especially if I had extra soup or a casserole. Lately I can still do those things, but they feel flatter. They don’t give me that little bit of warmth or satisfaction the way they used to. So yes, I suppose the interest is still there in a dutiful way, but the pleasure part has been missing.\n\nDoctor: When you feel at your lowest, are your thoughts mainly about missing your sister and her death?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to separate it out, honestly. A lot of the lowest moments do seem to have her in them, or at least her death is sort of the doorway into them, if that makes sense. I’ll think about something ordinary and then suddenly remember she isn’t here, and that can pull me down very quickly.\n\nBut I don’t know that I can say my thoughts are mainly only about missing her. Sometimes it feels more like this heavy feeling settles over everything, and then I may not even be thinking one clear thought. It’s more like… I’m just low, and if I try to trace it back, yes, her being gone is there. But it isn’t always a specific memory or a specific thought like “I miss my sister” running through my mind.\n\nSo I guess I’d say her death is very tied up in it, but when I’m at my lowest, it can feel broader and harder to name than just missing her.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have there still been moments when something can genuinely lift your mood, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean, there are moments where I can tell something should lift my mood, if that makes sense. Like I’ll still make myself go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night because I know it’s better than just staying home and withdrawing completely. And sometimes people are kind, or someone says something funny, and I can sort of recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to. I end up sitting there feeling a bit like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else be part of it. I’m present, but I’m not really… warmed by it, I suppose.\n\nEven with things that used to reliably give me a little lift, like being out in my garden or cooking something for the neighbors, it’s flatter now. I still do them sometimes, but that little spark of pleasure or satisfaction just doesn’t come the same way. So if my mood lifts, it’s brief and kind of faint, not the genuine lift I remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this low even when you are not thinking about your sister?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I wish I could give you a cleaner answer, but it’s hard for me to untangle it in the moment.\n\nThere are times when I’m low and I don’t think I’m consciously thinking, “This is about my sister,” or picturing her exactly. It can just feel like the day has gone dim somehow, and I’m moving through it with that heaviness there. But then if I stop and examine it, I wonder if she’s still underneath it in some way, even if I wasn’t actively thinking about her.\n\nSo… yes, I think I can feel low even when she isn’t right at the front of my mind. But I also don’t know how much she’s still in the background, if that makes sense. It’s not always a clear thought about her, but it may still be connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings begin only after your sister died?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, really only after she died. It’s been about a month now, maybe just a little over or under depending on how I count it, but that’s when all of this started in this way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad periods in life before, of course—at my age you don’t get through everything untouched—but this particular heaviness, the way it’s been sitting on me day after day, began after my sister died unexpectedly. It felt like one day life was difficult but still recognizable, and then after that call, or after realizing she was really gone, everything shifted. Since then it’s just been… about a month of feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have there been any moments when you truly felt better for a while, even if only briefly?\n\nPatient: Only in a very muted sort of way. I still make myself go to things, like book club or the dance night, because part of me knows those are the things that used to help me feel more like myself. And I don’t want to just disappear into the house and stop doing everything.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s not the same. I can be sitting with people I like, hearing the conversation, maybe even knowing something is funny or pleasant, but it feels like it doesn’t quite get through to me. Almost like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching it happen rather than really being in it.\n\nAnd that’s true at home too. My garden used to give me such a little lift, even just checking on things or pulling a few weeds, and cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and warm inside. Lately I still do those things sometimes, but the feeling I used to get from them just… doesn’t arrive. So I suppose there are small moments where I’m distracted or a bit steadier, but truly better? Not really, not in the way I remember.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one, no—not like this, and not clearly for two weeks or longer.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches. There were times when I felt discouraged or worn down, or when life felt lonely or disappointing for a while. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve never been sad before, because that wouldn’t be true. But when you ask “depressed like this,” I have trouble saying yes, because this feels different in its weight and how it’s stayed with me.\n\nSo… as best as I can answer, no, I don’t think I’ve had another period before this where I felt this way for two weeks or more. Not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you felt down or had little pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. It’s not always the same intensity every hour, but it’s been there most days in one form or another.\n\nI still make myself show up for things, like book club or the dance night, because I don’t want to just stop living my life completely, and those things used to mean something to me. But lately I’ll be sitting there, with people talking around me, and I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass. I can see that I’m supposed to be enjoying it, or I remember that I used to enjoy it, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nAnd it’s the same with the smaller things at home. My garden used to give me this little lift, even just looking at what was coming up or fussing with the plants. Cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and connected. Now I still do some of it, but the pleasure just isn’t there the way it was. So yes, I’d say it’s been nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you noticed a change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Sleep has gotten… broken, I guess is the best way to put it.\n\nI don’t usually have one solid stretch of sleep anymore. I’ll fall asleep for a little while, then come up again, then drift off again, almost in bits and pieces. And then very early, before it’s really morning—before dawn a lot of the time—I wake up and my mind is already going. Not necessarily in an organized way, just turning things over, and once that starts I usually can’t settle back down.\n\nI’ll lie there trying to be still, telling myself there’s no point getting up yet, but the day has sort of already begun in my head. By the time it’s actually morning, I feel like I never really got properly rested.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up feeling at your lowest, is your first thought usually about your sister being gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s usually that clear.\n\nSometimes, yes, my sister is there in my mind right away—or not even a thought exactly, more like I wake up and there’s this awareness that she’s still gone, if that makes sense. Like I haven’t had time to think anything yet, but the fact of it is already sitting there.\n\nBut other mornings it’s more vague than that. I wake up low, and it takes me a little while to know what I’m even feeling or why. It’s not always a sentence in my head like, “My sister is gone.” Sometimes it’s just heaviness first, and then the thoughts come in afterward.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say yes, that’s always or usually my first thought. It can be part of it, but I don’t know that I’ve tracked it closely enough to answer more definitely.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think guilt more than worthless, though sometimes the guilt kind of turns into feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nI keep going back over those last weeks with my sister, almost like I’m reviewing a tape in my head. I’ll think, why didn’t I call that day, or why didn’t I stay on the phone longer, or why didn’t I ask better questions? I tell myself I should have listened more carefully, or said something more comforting, or found some more useful way to help her instead of just doing whatever I did at the time.\n\nAnd part of me knows—at least rationally—that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I do know that. If someone else said these things to me, I’d probably tell them they were being too hard on themselves. But when it’s me, it doesn’t seem to land the same way. I keep thinking there must have been something I missed, something I should have done differently.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling guilty. Excessively, maybe… I don’t know. It feels excessive when I say it out loud, but it still feels very real when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel guilty, is it mostly about your sister and what happened around her death?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. I mean, that’s where my mind goes with it almost every time.\n\nIt’s those last weeks with my sister that I keep replaying. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or lying awake, and suddenly I’m back there thinking, I should have called her more. Or I should have stayed on the phone longer when I did call. I should have listened better, picked up on something, asked the right question, done something actually useful instead of just… whatever I thought was enough at the time.\n\nAnd I do understand, at least in one part of my mind, that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I can say that out loud. But it doesn’t stop the other part from going over it again and again, like if I review it carefully enough I’ll find the moment where I failed her. So yes, it’s mostly tied to her, and to what happened around her death.\n\nDoctor: When something genuinely good happens, does your mood lift at all in that moment?\n\nPatient: Sometimes it does, a little, but not the way it used to. That’s the part that’s been unsettling, honestly.\n\nI still make myself go to things. Like I’ll go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night if I said I would, because I know if I stop going entirely that probably isn’t good for me. And sometimes something nice is happening right in front of me—someone says something funny, or everyone’s talking about a book, or the music is on—and I can recognize, intellectually, that this is pleasant. I can even smile or laugh in the right place.\n\nBut inside it’s… muted. Like I’m there, but I’m behind a pane of glass watching it instead of really being in it. The good thing happens, and I see that it’s good, but it doesn’t quite reach me.\n\nEven things that used to give me that small lift—my garden, especially, or cooking something for the neighbors—lately I still do them, but the little spark I used to get from them isn’t really there. I might be glad I did it afterward in a kind of dutiful way, but it doesn’t brighten me the way it used to. So yes, maybe there’s a slight lift sometimes, but it feels very brief and far away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I do know that. If someone else said these things to me, I’d probably tell them they were being too hard on themselves. But when it’s me, it doesn’t seem to land the same way. I keep thinking there must have been something I missed, something I should have done differently.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling guilty. Excessively, maybe… I don’t know. It feels excessive when I say it out loud, but it still feels very real when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel guilty, is it mostly about your sister and what happened around her death?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. I mean, that’s where my mind goes with it almost every time.\n\nIt’s those last weeks with my sister that I keep replaying. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or lying awake, and suddenly I’m back there thinking, I should have called her more. Or I should have stayed on the phone longer when I did call. I should have listened better, picked up on something, asked the right question, done something actually useful instead of just… whatever I thought was enough at the time.\n\nAnd I do understand, at least in one part of my mind, that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I can say that out loud. But it doesn’t stop the other part from going over it again and again, like if I review it carefully enough I’ll find the moment where I failed her. So yes, it’s mostly tied to her, and to what happened around her death.\n\nDoctor: When something genuinely good happens, does your mood lift at all in that moment?\n\nPatient: Sometimes it does, a little, but not the way it used to. That’s the part that’s been unsettling, honestly.\n\nI still make myself go to things. Like I’ll go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night if I said I would, because I know if I stop going entirely that probably isn’t good for me. And sometimes something nice is happening right in front of me—someone says something funny, or everyone’s talking about a book, or the music is on—and I can recognize, intellectually, that this is pleasant. I can even smile or laugh in the right place.\n\nBut inside it’s… muted. Like I’m there, but I’m behind a pane of glass watching it instead of really being in it. The good thing happens, and I see that it’s good, but it doesn’t quite reach me.\n\nEven things that used to give me that small lift—my garden, especially, or cooking something for the neighbors—lately I still do them, but the little spark I used to get from them isn’t really there. I might be glad I did it afterward in a kind of dutiful way, but it doesn’t brighten me the way it used to. So yes, maybe there’s a slight lift sometimes, but it feels very brief and far away.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling worthless?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not worthless exactly… not all the time, anyway. It’s more that I feel like I failed her, and that can start to feel like something is wrong with me.

I keep going back over those last weeks with my sister and picking apart every little thing I did or didn’t do. I’ll think, why didn’t I call more often, why didn’t I stay on the phone longer, why didn’t I listen better? Maybe if I’d asked a different question, or noticed something in her voice, or done something more useful, then… I don’t know. I know I can’t really prove any of that, and part of me does understand I probably couldn’t have changed what happened.

But I still keep putting it on myself. So I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless” in those exact words, but I do get into this place where I feel like I wasn’t enough for her when it mattered.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, I suppose because I got frightened by my own thoughts. Some mornings, when I wake up, or even before I’m fully out of bed, I find myself thinking it might be easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Not that I have a plan to do anything to myself—I don’t—but the thought is there, and it’s been unsettling enough that I felt I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nI almost didn’t make the appointment because part of me kept saying, “Oh, you’re just being dramatic,” or “Everyone has low days,” but it’s the fact that it keeps coming back in the mornings that worries me. But I may need you to clarify what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I’m still doing some of the things I normally do, so from the outside it may not look like I’ve stopped enjoying life exactly. I can see that these are the things I used to enjoy, and I know how I’m supposed to feel, but I don’t really get that same pleasure from them.\n\nEven smaller things at home… my garden used to give me such a lift, just checking on what was coming up or fussing over the plants. And I used to really enjoy cooking something for the neighbors, especially if I had extra soup or a casserole. Lately I can still do those things, but they feel flatter. They don’t give me that little bit of warmth or satisfaction the way they used to. So yes, I suppose the interest is still there in a dutiful way, but the pleasure part has been missing.\n\nDoctor: When you feel at your lowest, are your thoughts mainly about missing your sister and her death?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to separate it out, honestly. A lot of the lowest moments do seem to have her in them, or at least her death is sort of the doorway into them, if that makes sense. It’s more like… I’m just low, and if I try to trace it back, yes, her being gone is there. I mean, there are moments where I can tell something should lift my mood, if that makes sense. Like I’ll still make myself go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night because I know it’s better than just staying home and withdrawing completely. And sometimes people are kind, or someone says something funny, and I can sort of recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to. I end up sitting there feeling a bit like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else be part of it. I’m present, but I’m not really… warmed by it, I suppose.\n\nEven with things that used to reliably give me a little lift, like being out in my garden or cooking something for the neighbors, it’s flatter now. I still do them sometimes, but that little spark of pleasure or satisfaction just doesn’t come the same way. It felt like one day life was difficult but still recognizable, and then after that call, or after realizing she was really gone, everything shifted. Since then it’s just been… about a month of feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have there been any moments when you truly felt better for a while, even if only briefly?\n\nPatient: Only in a very muted sort of way. I still make myself go to things, like book club or the dance night, because part of me knows those are the things that used to help me feel more like myself. And I don’t want to just disappear into the house and stop doing everything.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s not the same. I can be sitting with people I like, hearing the conversation, maybe even knowing something is funny or pleasant, but it feels like it doesn’t quite get through to me. My garden used to give me such a little lift, even just checking on things or pulling a few weeds, and cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and warm inside. Lately I still do those things sometimes, but the feeling I used to get from them just… doesn’t arrive. So I suppose there are small moments where I’m distracted or a bit steadier, but truly better? Not really, not in the way I remember.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one, no—not like this, and not clearly for two weeks or longer.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches. There were times when I felt discouraged or worn down, or when life felt lonely or disappointing for a while. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve never been sad before, because that wouldn’t be true. But when you ask “depressed like this,” I have trouble saying yes, because this feels different in its weight and how it’s stayed with me.\n\nSo… as best as I can answer, no, I don’t think I’ve had another period before this where I felt this way for two weeks or more. Not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you felt down or had little pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. I wake up low, and it takes me a little while to know what I’m even feeling or why. I tell myself I should have listened more carefully, or said something more comforting, or found some more useful way to help her instead of just doing whatever I did at the time.\n\nAnd part of me knows—at least rationally—that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I do know that. If someone else said these things to me, I’d probably tell them they were being too hard on themselves. But when it’s me, it doesn’t seem to land the same way. I keep thinking there must have been something I missed, something I should have done differently.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling guilty. Excessively, maybe… I don’t know. Some mornings, when I wake up, or even before I’m fully out of bed, I find myself thinking it might be easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Not that I have a plan to do anything to myself—I don’t—but the thought is there, and it’s been unsettling enough that I felt I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nI almost didn’t make the appointment because part of me kept saying, “Oh, you’re just being dramatic,” or “Everyone has low days,” but it’s the fact that it keeps coming back in the mornings that worries me. I don’t want to hurt myself, but I also don’t feel right pretending those thoughts aren’t happening. So… that’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer “most days,” if I’m honest. I know that sounds evasive, but I don’t mean it that way. I’ve certainly had mornings where I wake up with this heavy, low feeling, and then I spend part of the day sort of moving through things without much brightness in me.\n\nBut whether it’s truly most days, I haven’t been keeping track in any careful way. Some days I can still function and seem fairly normal from the outside, so I start questioning whether I’m really “depressed” or just having a rough patch. I suppose I’d say I’ve been feeling down more often than I’d like, but I’m not sure I can confidently say exactly how many days.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It started after my sister died—very suddenly, unexpectedly—and since then I haven’t really felt like I’ve gotten my footing back.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself that of course I’m upset, anyone would be, but it’s been about four weeks now and the heaviness has sort of stayed with me. I don’t mean every single minute of every day, but since her death, that’s when I started noticing this change in myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been worrying excessively about a lot of different things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “a lot of different things,” no. I mean, I do worry, but I don’t know that it’s this broad, constant worrying about everything, if that’s what you mean.\n\nLately my thoughts tend to circle around certain things, and then I second-guess myself about whether that counts as excessive or whether it’s just… understandable. I wouldn’t say I’m lying awake making lists of every possible disaster, but I also don’t feel especially settled. It’s a little hard for me to separate worry from just feeling unsettled and sad, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I suppose my answer is: not really a wide range of worries, at least not that I’ve noticed. But I may need you to clarify what you mean by excessive.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I’m still doing some of the things I normally do, so from the outside it may not look like I’ve stopped enjoying life exactly. I still make myself go to book club, for example, and I’ve gone to the dance night a couple of times because I don’t want to disappear or have people worry about me.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s like I’m not fully in the room. I’m sitting there while everyone is talking or laughing, and I feel sort of behind a pane of glass, if that makes sense. I can see that these are the things I used to enjoy, and I know how I’m supposed to feel, but I don’t really get that same pleasure from them.\n\nEven smaller things at home… my garden used to give me such a lift, just checking on what was coming up or fussing over the plants. And I used to really enjoy cooking something for the neighbors, especially if I had extra soup or a casserole. Lately I can still do those things, but they feel flatter. They don’t give me that little bit of warmth or satisfaction the way they used to. So yes, I suppose the interest is still there in a dutiful way, but the pleasure part has been missing.\n\nDoctor: When you feel at your lowest, are your thoughts mainly about missing your sister and her death?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to separate it out, honestly. A lot of the lowest moments do seem to have her in them, or at least her death is sort of the doorway into them, if that makes sense. I’ll think about something ordinary and then suddenly remember she isn’t here, and that can pull me down very quickly.\n\nBut I don’t know that I can say my thoughts are mainly only about missing her. Sometimes it feels more like this heavy feeling settles over everything, and then I may not even be thinking one clear thought. It’s more like… I’m just low, and if I try to trace it back, yes, her being gone is there. But it isn’t always a specific memory or a specific thought like “I miss my sister” running through my mind.\n\nSo I guess I’d say her death is very tied up in it, but when I’m at my lowest, it can feel broader and harder to name than just missing her.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have there still been moments when something can genuinely lift your mood, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean, there are moments where I can tell something should lift my mood, if that makes sense. Like I’ll still make myself go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night because I know it’s better than just staying home and withdrawing completely. And sometimes people are kind, or someone says something funny, and I can sort of recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to. I end up sitting there feeling a bit like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else be part of it. I’m present, but I’m not really… warmed by it, I suppose.\n\nEven with things that used to reliably give me a little lift, like being out in my garden or cooking something for the neighbors, it’s flatter now. I still do them sometimes, but that little spark of pleasure or satisfaction just doesn’t come the same way. So if my mood lifts, it’s brief and kind of faint, not the genuine lift I remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this low even when you are not thinking about your sister?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I wish I could give you a cleaner answer, but it’s hard for me to untangle it in the moment.\n\nThere are times when I’m low and I don’t think I’m consciously thinking, “This is about my sister,” or picturing her exactly. It can just feel like the day has gone dim somehow, and I’m moving through it with that heaviness there. But then if I stop and examine it, I wonder if she’s still underneath it in some way, even if I wasn’t actively thinking about her.\n\nSo… yes, I think I can feel low even when she isn’t right at the front of my mind. But I also don’t know how much she’s still in the background, if that makes sense. It’s not always a clear thought about her, but it may still be connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings begin only after your sister died?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, really only after she died. It’s been about a month now, maybe just a little over or under depending on how I count it, but that’s when all of this started in this way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad periods in life before, of course—at my age you don’t get through everything untouched—but this particular heaviness, the way it’s been sitting on me day after day, began after my sister died unexpectedly. It felt like one day life was difficult but still recognizable, and then after that call, or after realizing she was really gone, everything shifted. Since then it’s just been… about a month of feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have there been any moments when you truly felt better for a while, even if only briefly?\n\nPatient: Only in a very muted sort of way. I still make myself go to things, like book club or the dance night, because part of me knows those are the things that used to help me feel more like myself. And I don’t want to just disappear into the house and stop doing everything.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s not the same. I can be sitting with people I like, hearing the conversation, maybe even knowing something is funny or pleasant, but it feels like it doesn’t quite get through to me. Almost like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching it happen rather than really being in it.\n\nAnd that’s true at home too. My garden used to give me such a little lift, even just checking on things or pulling a few weeds, and cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and warm inside. Lately I still do those things sometimes, but the feeling I used to get from them just… doesn’t arrive. So I suppose there are small moments where I’m distracted or a bit steadier, but truly better? Not really, not in the way I remember.\n\nDoctor: Before your sister died, had you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to one, no—not like this, and not clearly for two weeks or longer.\n\nI mean, I’ve had hard stretches. There were times when I felt discouraged or worn down, or when life felt lonely or disappointing for a while. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve never been sad before, because that wouldn’t be true. But when you ask “depressed like this,” I have trouble saying yes, because this feels different in its weight and how it’s stayed with me.\n\nSo… as best as I can answer, no, I don’t think I’ve had another period before this where I felt this way for two weeks or more. Not that I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you felt down or had little pleasure in things nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I think. It’s not always the same intensity every hour, but it’s been there most days in one form or another.\n\nI still make myself show up for things, like book club or the dance night, because I don’t want to just stop living my life completely, and those things used to mean something to me. But lately I’ll be sitting there, with people talking around me, and I feel like I’m behind a pane of glass. I can see that I’m supposed to be enjoying it, or I remember that I used to enjoy it, but it doesn’t really reach me.\n\nAnd it’s the same with the smaller things at home. My garden used to give me this little lift, even just looking at what was coming up or fussing with the plants. Cooking for the neighbors used to make me feel useful and connected. Now I still do some of it, but the pleasure just isn’t there the way it was. So yes, I’d say it’s been nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you noticed a change in your sleep?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. Sleep has gotten… broken, I guess is the best way to put it.\n\nI don’t usually have one solid stretch of sleep anymore. I’ll fall asleep for a little while, then come up again, then drift off again, almost in bits and pieces. And then very early, before it’s really morning—before dawn a lot of the time—I wake up and my mind is already going. Not necessarily in an organized way, just turning things over, and once that starts I usually can’t settle back down.\n\nI’ll lie there trying to be still, telling myself there’s no point getting up yet, but the day has sort of already begun in my head. By the time it’s actually morning, I feel like I never really got properly rested.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up feeling at your lowest, is your first thought usually about your sister being gone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it’s usually that clear.\n\nSometimes, yes, my sister is there in my mind right away—or not even a thought exactly, more like I wake up and there’s this awareness that she’s still gone, if that makes sense. Like I haven’t had time to think anything yet, but the fact of it is already sitting there.\n\nBut other mornings it’s more vague than that. I wake up low, and it takes me a little while to know what I’m even feeling or why. It’s not always a sentence in my head like, “My sister is gone.” Sometimes it’s just heaviness first, and then the thoughts come in afterward.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say yes, that’s always or usually my first thought. It can be part of it, but I don’t know that I’ve tracked it closely enough to answer more definitely.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think guilt more than worthless, though sometimes the guilt kind of turns into feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nI keep going back over those last weeks with my sister, almost like I’m reviewing a tape in my head. I’ll think, why didn’t I call that day, or why didn’t I stay on the phone longer, or why didn’t I ask better questions? I tell myself I should have listened more carefully, or said something more comforting, or found some more useful way to help her instead of just doing whatever I did at the time.\n\nAnd part of me knows—at least rationally—that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I do know that. If someone else said these things to me, I’d probably tell them they were being too hard on themselves. But when it’s me, it doesn’t seem to land the same way. I keep thinking there must have been something I missed, something I should have done differently.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling guilty. Excessively, maybe… I don’t know. It feels excessive when I say it out loud, but it still feels very real when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: When you feel guilty, is it mostly about your sister and what happened around her death?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. I mean, that’s where my mind goes with it almost every time.\n\nIt’s those last weeks with my sister that I keep replaying. I’ll be doing something ordinary, or lying awake, and suddenly I’m back there thinking, I should have called her more. Or I should have stayed on the phone longer when I did call. I should have listened better, picked up on something, asked the right question, done something actually useful instead of just… whatever I thought was enough at the time.\n\nAnd I do understand, at least in one part of my mind, that I probably couldn’t have changed what happened. I can say that out loud. But it doesn’t stop the other part from going over it again and again, like if I review it carefully enough I’ll find the moment where I failed her. So yes, it’s mostly tied to her, and to what happened around her death.\n\nDoctor: When something genuinely good happens, does your mood lift at all in that moment?\n\nPatient: Sometimes it does, a little, but not the way it used to. That’s the part that’s been unsettling, honestly.\n\nI still make myself go to things. Like I’ll go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night if I said I would, because I know if I stop going entirely that probably isn’t good for me. And sometimes something nice is happening right in front of me—someone says something funny, or everyone’s talking about a book, or the music is on—and I can recognize, intellectually, that this is pleasant. I can even smile or laugh in the right place.\n\nBut inside it’s… muted. Like I’m there, but I’m behind a pane of glass watching it instead of really being in it. The good thing happens, and I see that it’s good, but it doesn’t quite reach me.\n\nEven things that used to give me that small lift—my garden, especially, or cooking something for the neighbors—lately I still do them, but the little spark I used to get from them isn’t really there. I might be glad I did it afterward in a kind of dutiful way, but it doesn’t brighten me the way it used to. So yes, maybe there’s a slight lift sometimes, but it feels very brief and far away.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Not worthless exactly… not all the time, anyway. It’s more that I feel like I failed her, and that can start to feel like something is wrong with me.\n\nI keep going back over those last weeks with my sister and picking apart every little thing I did or didn’t do. I’ll think, why didn’t I call more often, why didn’t I stay on the phone longer, why didn’t I listen better? Maybe if I’d asked a different question, or noticed something in her voice, or done something more useful, then… I don’t know. I know I can’t really prove any of that, and part of me does understand I probably couldn’t have changed what happened.\n\nBut I still keep putting it on myself. So I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless” in those exact words, but I do get into this place where I feel like I wasn’t enough for her when it mattered.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Hopeless… I’m not completely sure how to answer that.

I don’t think I walk around thinking, “There is no hope,” in a clear way like that. It’s more that sometimes everything feels very heavy and hard to imagine changing. But I don’t know if that’s hopelessness exactly, or just being worn down and sad and stuck in the same thoughts.

There are moments when I can still picture things being a little easier someday, or at least I tell myself they might be. So I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless all the time. It’s just… I don’t feel very confident right now either, if that makes sense.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… well, I suppose because I got frightened by my own thoughts. Some mornings, when I wake up, or even before I’m fully out of bed, I find myself thinking it might be easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Not that I have a plan to do anything to myself—I don’t—but the thought is there, and it’s been unsettling enough that I felt I shouldn’t just keep brushing it aside.\n\nI almost didn’t make the appointment because part of me kept saying, “Oh, you’re just being dramatic,” or “Everyone has low days,” but it’s the fact that it keeps coming back in the mornings that worries me. I don’t want to hurt myself, but I also don’t feel right pretending those thoughts aren’t happening. It started after my sister died—very suddenly, unexpectedly—and since then I haven’t really felt like I’ve gotten my footing back.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself that of course I’m upset, anyone would be, but it’s been about four weeks now and the heaviness has sort of stayed with me. But it isn’t always a specific memory or a specific thought like “I miss my sister” running through my mind.\n\nSo I guess I’d say her death is very tied up in it, but when I’m at my lowest, it can feel broader and harder to name than just missing her.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have there still been moments when something can genuinely lift your mood, even briefly?\n\nPatient: Not very often, no. I mean, there are moments where I can tell something should lift my mood, if that makes sense. Like I’ll still make myself go to book club, or I’ll go to the dance night because I know it’s better than just staying home and withdrawing completely. And sometimes people are kind, or someone says something funny, and I can sort of recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut it doesn’t really reach me the way it used to. I end up sitting there feeling a bit like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching everyone else be part of it. I’m present, but I’m not really… warmed by it, I suppose.\n\nEven with things that used to reliably give me a little lift, like being out in my garden or cooking something for the neighbors, it’s flatter now. I still do them sometimes, but that little spark of pleasure or satisfaction just doesn’t come the same way. So if my mood lifts, it’s brief and kind of faint, not the genuine lift I remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this low even when you are not thinking about your sister?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I wish I could give you a cleaner answer, but it’s hard for me to untangle it in the moment.\n\nThere are times when I’m low and I don’t think I’m consciously thinking, “This is about my sister,” or picturing her exactly. It can just feel like the day has gone dim somehow, and I’m moving through it with that heaviness there. But then if I stop and examine it, I wonder if she’s still underneath it in some way, even if I wasn’t actively thinking about her.\n\nSo… yes, I think I can feel low even when she isn’t right at the front of my mind. But I also don’t know how much she’s still in the background, if that makes sense. It’s not always a clear thought about her, but it may still be connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings begin only after your sister died?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, really only after she died. It’s been about a month now, maybe just a little over or under depending on how I count it, but that’s when all of this started in this way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had sad periods in life before, of course—at my age you don’t get through everything untouched—but this particular heaviness, the way it’s been sitting on me day after day, began after my sister died unexpectedly. It felt like one day life was difficult but still recognizable, and then after that call, or after realizing she was really gone, everything shifted. Since then it’s just been… about a month of feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Since this began, have there been any moments when you truly felt better for a while, even if only briefly?\n\nPatient: Only in a very muted sort of way. I still make myself go to things, like book club or the dance night, because part of me knows those are the things that used to help me feel more like myself. And I don’t want to just disappear into the house and stop doing everything.\n\nBut when I’m there, it’s not the same. I can be sitting with people I like, hearing the conversation, maybe even knowing something is funny or pleasant, but it feels like it doesn’t quite get through to me. Almost like I’m behind a pane of glass, watching it happen rather than really being in it.\n\nAnd that’s true at home too.
